Notations

4.3
Related Work

We briefly review a few related lines of prior work on neural collaborative filtering and long-tail item recommendations with interaction sparsity.

Neural Collaborative Filtering
:
The core paradigm of latent-factor CF models is to parameterize users and items with latent representations learned from historical user-item interactions.
Recent neural recommenders enhance the representational capacity of CF models via non-linear latent representations
[
89
,
90
]
, neural interaction modeling
[
88
,
94
]
, and graph-based representation learning
[
91
]
.
While these neural recommenders learn expressive models to significantly outperform conventional CF approaches, sparsity concerns owing to long-tail items remain a critical challenge.

Sparsity-aware Recommendation:

Clustering is one popular way to address interaction sparsity by modeling group-level purchasing behaviors; early methods generate recommendations for tail items at the granularity of item clusters
[
95
]
,
e.g.
, cluster-based smoothing
[
196
]
, user-item co-clustering
[
96
]
and joint clustering and collaborative filtering
[
97
]
. However, clustering in the presence of distributional skew can lead to uninformative results
[
197
]
and degrades the extent of personalization.

Recent techniques leverage
item-item co-occurrence
statistics and
distributional priors
to regularize latent-factor CF methods. One common approach is
regularization
of recommendation models,
e.g.
, factorization of an item co-occurrence matrix that shares latent factors with a CF model
[
193
,
194
]
. Variational auto-encoders (VAEs)
[
90
,
98
,
179
]
employ multivariate Gaussian priors as regularizers on the representational space to handle sparsity.
Another strategy to alleviate sparsity is
data augmentation
for items (or users) in the tail via
adversarial
regularization
[
9
]
or rating generation
[
99
,
198
]
techniques.
However, regularization techniques typically impose static hypotheses with restrictive assumptions, while adversarial learning is computationally expensive and does not scale to massive item inventories.

Cold-start recommendation is a related problem that targets
new
users or
new
items with
no
historical interactions.
In such a scenario, models rely on auxiliary information,
e.g.
, user profiles
[
110
]
, item content
[
195
]
, social connections
[
106
,
171
,
199
]
, and knowledge graphs
[
107
]
.
An effective strategy is randomized feature dropouts to enable generalization to missing inputs
[
108
,
109
]
.
In contrast, we focus on
few-shot
recommendations over long-tail items with very few interactions,
without
any auxiliary information.

Few-shot Learning:
this learning paradigm involves designing models capable of learning new tasks rapidly given
a limited number of training examples.
Here, the
meta-learning
(learning to learn) paradigm has achieved considerable success in several domains including computer vision
[
100
,
101
]
, natural language
[
200
]
, and data mining
[
110
,
116
]
.

A few recent methods adapt meta-learning to cold-start recommendation
[
201
]
.
Prior meta-learning work have examined gradient-based parameter
[
110
,
111
,
112
,
113
,
114
]
and hyper-parameter initialization
[
115
]
, and layer sharing with user-specific (or item) parameter adaptation
[
116
,
117
,
118
]
. However, these methods mostly address the cold-start (zero-shot) scenario with side information, thus inapplicable to our general setting of few-shot recommendation given limited interactions.

Our work is conceptually related to
metric-learning
approaches
[
100
,
101
,
202
]
for few-shot learning, which
learn-to-learn
a metric space that generalizes to new tasks without any need for adaptation.
A few recent explorations address task heterogeneity
[
203
]
in few-shot classification settings, by designing architectures for explicit knowledge transfer from data-rich classes
[
204
,
205
,
206
]
to construct few-shot classifiers.
However, such techniques are suited to learning classifiers from a
limited
number of classes, hence cannot scale to handle
massive
item inventories in recommendation applications.
To our knowledge, ours is the
first
investigation of metric-based few-shot learning for long-tail item recommendations.

4.4
ProtoCF Framework

Learning informative representations for tail items poses the key challenge of
interaction sparsity
,
i.e.
, each tail item only has a few observed interactions.
Without access to side information (
e.g.
, item content or contextual attributes), we critically remark that prior neural recommenders
[
90
,
89
]
learn informative representations for head items with abundant training interactions.
Thus, to learn discriminative tail item representations, we propose a novel few-shot learning framework
ProtoCF
with two key steps:

First, we train a
base recommender

{\bm{R}}_{B}
to learn high-quality user representations that mainly capture preferences over head items and infer item-item relationships (Section
4.4.1
).
Then, we present a
few-shot recommender

{\bm{R}}_{F}
that
extracts and transfers
knowledge from the base recommender
{\bm{R}}_{B}
to
learn-to-recommend
few-shot items (Sections
4.4.2
,
4.4.3
).

Figure 4.3:
Outline of the different components of a neural base recommender based on collaborative filtering.

4.4.1
Neural Base Recommender

In this section, we outline the architecture of a differentiable base recommender
{\bm{R}}_{B}
to learn a ranking
f_{\phi}(i\mid u),i\in{\mathcal{I}}
(with parameters
\phi
) over the interactions
{\bm{X}}
. Neural CF models
[
90
,
88
,
89
]
typically learn latent representations for users and
items, followed by an interaction function and learning objective for model training.

User and Item Representations

Latent-factor CF methods adopt a variety of representation learning strategies, including matrix factorization
[
85
,
87
]
, autoencoders
[
90
,
89
]
and graph neural networks
[
91
]
. We define preference encoders
F_{U}(\cdot\mid\phi)
and
F_{I}(\cdot\mid\phi)
to learn user
{\mathbf{h}}_{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
and item
{\mathbf{h}}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
embeddings for user
u\in{\mathcal{U}}
and item
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
, which can be described by:

{\mathbf{h}}_{u}=F_{U}(u,{\bm{X}}\mid\phi)\hskip 15.0pt{\mathbf{h}}_{i}=F_{I}(i,{\bm{X}}\mid\phi)

(4.1)

Training Objective

We define a user-item interaction function
F_{\textsc{int}}(\cdot\mid\phi)
to compute a prediction score
\hat{y}_{b}(u,i)
that indicates the relevance of item
i
to user
u
. The training objective
L_{B}
of the base recommender
{\bm{R}}_{B}
is given by:

\hat{y}_{b}(u,i)=F_{\textsc{int}}({\mathbf{h}}_{u},{\mathbf{h}}_{i}\mid\phi)\hskip 15.0ptL_{B}=l(\hat{y}_{b}(u,i),y_{ui}))

(4.2)

where
y_{ui}=1
for observed
(u,i)
pairs and
l(\cdot)
is a pairwise
[
87
]
or pointwise
[
88
]
loss function. over the user-item interactions.
The interaction function
F_{\textsc{int}}
measures user-item relevance and is typically modeled using an inner product
[
87
,
89
,
90
,
91
]
.

We train the base recommender
{\bm{R}}_{B}
using cosine similarity since normalized representations generalize better to few-shot settings
[
207
]
(compared to unnormalized inner products), and facilitate unified recommendation of head and tail items during model inference. We denote the
item-item proximity

\text{sim}_{b}(\cdot)
in the latent space of
{\bm{R}}_{B}
by:

p_{B}(i,j)\propto\text{sim}_{b}({\mathbf{h}}_{i},{\mathbf{h}}_{j})=\text{cos}({\mathbf{h}}_{i},{\mathbf{h}}_{j})\hskip 15.0pti,j\in{\mathcal{I}}

(4.3)

We pre-train
{\bm{R}}_{B}
to extract knowledge of user preferences over head items via encoder
F_{U}(\cdot)
and item-item proximities among different items via
p_{B}(i,j)
.
Below, we present our proposed framework
ProtoCF
that transfers knowledge from the
data-rich
head to the
data-poor
tail to enable robust few-shot recommendations.

4.4.2
Few-shot Item Recommendation

In this section, we formulate long-tail item recommendation as
few-shot item representation learning
for items given a small
support set
of upto
K
interacted users for each tail item (typically
K\approx 5\text{ to }20
interactions).

Few-shot Task Formulation

Our framework is grounded on
episodic
learning
[
100
]
with a collection of
meta-training
tasks or episodes.
One approach
[
101
,
207
]
is to construct meta-training tasks from the interactions of data-rich head items;
however, excluding the diverse collection of tail items may impede generalization.
Thus, our meta-training tasks operate on the entire item set
{\mathcal{I}}_{T}
.

Figure 4.4:
Episodic few-shot learning with meta-training task
{\mathcal{T}}
and item embedding inference at meta-testing.

Each meta-training task
{\mathcal{T}}
is a personalized ranking problem over a subset of
N
items
{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
randomly sampled from
{\mathcal{I}}
. We
simulate the interaction distribution of few-shot items during meta-training
by sampling
K
training interactions (from
N_{i}
) for each item
i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
in task
{\mathcal{T}}
.
During inference, we generate
few-shot
item recommendations from samples of upto
K
training interactions per item.
By ensuring consistency between meta-training and inference, we bridge the
distribution mismatch
between head and tail items. The meta-knowledge extracted across diverse meta-training tasks benefits tail items with sparse interactions.

The episodic few-shot training process operates over a collection of meta-training tasks
\{{\mathcal{T}}_{1},{\mathcal{T}}_{2},\dots\}
sampled from a task distribution
p({\mathbb{T}})
over the item set
{\mathcal{I}}
. Specifically, a
K
-shot,
N
-item
meta-training task
{\mathcal{T}}
sampled from
p({\mathbb{T}})
consists of support
{\mathcal{S}}
and query
{\mathcal{Q}}
user sets over
N
items
{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}\subset{\mathcal{I}}
(analogous to the usual sense of training and testing sets respectively). Each meta-training task
{\mathcal{T}}\sim p({\mathbb{T}})
is defined by:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}

\displaystyle=\{{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N},{\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{Q}}\}\qquad\hskip 8.0pt{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}\subset{\mathcal{I}}

(4.4)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle=\{{\mathcal{S}}_{i}:i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}\}\qquad{\mathcal{S}}_{i}=\{u_{i,1},\dots,u_{i,K}\}\qquad

\displaystyle u_{i,k}\in N_{i}\quad

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}

\displaystyle=\{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}:i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}\}\qquad{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}=\{u^{\prime}_{i,1},\dots,u^{\prime}_{i,K^{\prime}}\}

\displaystyle u^{\prime}_{i,k^{\prime}}\in N_{i}\quad

where the support set
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
contains
K
interacted users sampled for each item
i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
and the corresponding query set
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
includes
K^{\prime}
interacted users sampled for each of the
N
items.

We learn a
few-shot recommender

{\bm{R}}_{F}
that takes as input the support users
{\mathcal{S}}
to
learn-to-compose
representations for items
i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
in task
{\mathcal{T}}
.
The few-shot recommender
{\bm{R}}_{F}
is trained by optimizing a learning objective designed to match the item recommendations generated by
{\bm{R}}_{F}
for its query users
{\mathcal{Q}}
with their corresponding ground-truth interactions over the item
{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
in each meta-training task
{\mathcal{T}}
.

Initial Item Prototype

We learn a shared
metric space
of users and items to synthesize a representation for each item
i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
based on its support set
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
(with
K
interactions) in task
{\mathcal{T}}
.
The metric space (with similarity
\text{sim}_{m}(\cdot)
) compactly clusters the interacted users
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
of each item
i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
around a
prototype
representation
{\mathbf{p}}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}

[
101
]
.

We first define a user preference encoder
G_{U}(\cdot\mid\theta)
that maps each user
u\in{\mathcal{U}}
into a latent embedding space.
To directly transfer knowledge from the base recommender
{\bm{R}}_{B}
, the few-shot user encoder
G_{U}(\cdot\mid\theta)
has model parameters initialized from its pre-trained encoder
F_{U}(\cdot\mid\phi)
in the base recommender
{\bm{R}}_{B}
(Equation
4.1
), but is parameterized with learnable parameters
\theta
.
The prototype
{\mathbf{p}}_{i}
for item
i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
is computed as the mean vector of the embedded support user set
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
, defined by:

{\mathbf{p}}_{i}=\frac{1}{{\mathcal{S}}_{i}}\sum\limits_{u_{i,k}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}}G_{U}(u_{i,k},{\bm{X}}_{H}\mid\theta)\qquad i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}

(4.5)

Equation
4.5
encourages the prototype
{\mathbf{p}}_{i}
to learn a representative vector summarizing its cluster of interacted users.
However, we face two critical challenges in handling tail items:
first, due to sparse support sets, the prototypes are often
noisy
and sensitive to
outliers
;
second, due to substantial
heterogeneity
in the tail, simplistic averaging may lack the
resolution
to discriminate across diverse tail items.
Thus, the few-shot recommender
{\bm{R}}_{F}
needs a strong
inductive bias
during prototype learning to avoid overfitting, and yet have sufficient expressivity to learn
discriminative
prototypes.

Head-Tail Meta Knowledge Transfer

We now exploit the
item-to-item
relationship knowledge acquired by the neural base recommender
{\bm{R}}_{B}
(Section
4.4.1
) as an inductive bias to enhance the item prototype
{\mathbf{p}}_{i}
(Equation
4.5
).
Given an item
i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
with limited support users, extracting knowledge from the most related items to item
i
(based on the item-to-item proximities) can provide valuable guidance to synthesize robust item prototypes.

However, direct knowledge transfer from the base recommender
{\bm{R}}_{B}
to the few-shot recommender
{\bm{R}}_{F}
is technically challenging: dynamically identifying related items for each few-shot item during prototype construction is not scalable due to the arbitrary complexity of
{\bm{R}}_{B}
and the high cardinality of item sets in massive inventories.
Thus, we extract a
compact
representation of the
item-item proximity
knowledge (Equation
4.3
) discovered by
{\bm{R}}_{B}
and transfer this knowledge to enhance item prototypes.

Figure 4.5:
Group embeddings can be viewed as centroids of overlapping item clusters,
e.g.
, Cafe (category) or Chicago (location); items (Cafes located in Chicago) may often belong to overlapping item clusters.

Group-Enhanced Item Prototype Learning.

We learn a set of
M
group embeddings
{\mathcal{Z}}_{M}
as
basis vectors
modeling
item-item proximity
in the latent space of the base recommender
{\bm{R}}_{B}
,
i.e.
, if
p_{B}(i,j)
is high for a pair of items
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}
, then items
i
and
j
are likely to exhibit similar characteristics.
Formally, the set of group embeddings
{\mathcal{Z}}_{M}
are defined by the following equation below:

{\mathcal{Z}}_{M}=\{z_{m}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}:1\leq m\leq M\}\qquad M\ll|{\mathcal{I}}|

(4.6)

As depicted in Figure
4.5
, we intuitively visualize the group embeddings as
discriminative centroids
of
overlapping
clusters of items identified by
{\bm{R}}_{B}
, and conversely view items as
mixtures
over the group embeddings,
e.g.
, each centroid may represent a contextual factor such as a restaurant type (Cafe) or location (Chicago), while restaurants are mixture of multiple centroids (Cafe located in Chicago) and belong to overlapping item clusters.

To enhance the prototype
{\mathbf{p}}_{i}
of item
i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
, we synthesize a
group-enhanced prototype

{\mathbf{g}}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
as a mixture over the
M
group embeddings.
The mixture coefficients are estimated by a learnable attention mechanism
[
130
]
to measure
compatibility

\alpha_{im}
between the prototype
{\mathbf{p}}_{i}
and each centroid
z_{m}\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{M}
.
We parameterize the attention function with a lightweight network comprised of an auxiliary set
{\mathcal{K}}_{M}=\{{\mathbf{k}}_{m}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}:1\leq m\leq M\}
of trainable keys to index the group embeddings.
We implement the attention function with an inner product followed by softmax normalization, as:

{\mathbf{g}}_{i}=\sum\limits_{m=1}^{M}\alpha_{im}{\mathbf{z}}_{m}\qquad\alpha_{im}=\frac{\exp\big({\bm{W}}_{q}{\mathbf{p}}_{i}\cdot{\mathbf{k}}_{m}\big)}{\sum_{m^{\prime}=1}^{M}\exp\big({\bm{W}}_{q}{\mathbf{p}}_{i}\cdot{\mathbf{k}}_{m^{\prime}}\big)}

(4.7)

where
{\bm{W}}_{q}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D\times D}
is projects the prototype
{\mathbf{p}}_{i}
into a query to index the centroids.
The group-enhanced item prototype
{\mathbf{g}}_{i}
relates the different centroids via attention, transferring knowledge to few-shot items with sparse support sets.

Task-level Stochastic Knowledge Distillation.

We present a
knowledge distillation
strategy
[
208
]
to learn compact
group embeddings

{\mathcal{Z}}_{M}
that capture
item-item relationships
in
{\bm{R}}_{B}
.
We transfer knowledge from a high-capacity
teacher
model (base recommender
{\bm{R}}_{B}
) to a compact
student
model (group embeddings
{\mathcal{Z}}_{M}
) by encouraging the student to emulate predictions of the teacher;
{\mathcal{Z}}_{M}
is trained to emulate the item proximity distribution in
{\bm{R}}_{B}
.

Aligning pairwise item proximities over all items in
{\mathcal{I}}
is not scalable; thus, we operate at the granularity of each meta-training task
{\mathcal{T}}
.
For each item
i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
, we compute a soft probability distribution
p_{B}(j\mid i,{\bm{R}}_{B})
for the teacher model
{\bm{R}}_{B}
(based on equation
4.3
) over other items
j\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
in the task
{\mathcal{T}}
, described by:

p_{B}(j\mid i,{\bm{R}}_{B})=\frac{\exp\big(p_{B}(i,j)/T\big)}{\sum_{k\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}}\exp\big(p_{B}(i,k)/T\big)}\quad i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}

(4.8)

where
T>0
is a temperature scaling hyper-parameter to regulate the rate of knowledge transfer.
We analogously define the item similarity distribution
p_{F}(j\mid i,{\bm{Z}}_{M})
for the student model
{\mathcal{Z}}_{M}
based on the metric space proximity
\text{sim}_{m}(\cdot)
of group-enhanced prototypes
{\mathbf{g}}_{i}
and
{\mathbf{g}}_{j}
for items
i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
, defined by:

p_{F}(j\mid i,{\bm{Z}}_{M})=\frac{\exp\big(\text{sim}_{m}({\mathbf{g}}_{i},{\mathbf{g}}_{j})\big)}{\sum_{k\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}}\exp\big(\text{sim}_{m}({\mathbf{g}}_{i},{\mathbf{g}}_{k})\big)}\;i,j\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}

(4.9)

We align the two distributions by minimizing cross-entropy between their task-level similarities
[
208
]
.
Since each item is typically related to very few items within the task
{\mathcal{T}}
, our
stochastic knowledge distillation
loss
L_{G}
minimizes distribution divergence over the top-
n
(
n\approx 10
) related items (out of
N
) identified by the teacher
{\bm{R}}_{B}
, by:

L_{G}=-\frac{1}{nN}\sum\limits_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}}\sum\limits_{j\in\Top_{B,n}(i)}p_{B}(j\mid i,{\bm{R}}_{B})\log p_{F}(j\mid i,{\bm{Z}}_{M})

(4.10)

where
{\Top}_{B,n}(i)=\text{Top}_{n}\big(p_{B}(\cdot\mid i,{\bm{R}}_{B})\big)
denotes the top-
n
most related items to item
i
within
{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
based on the teacher
{\bm{R}}_{B}
.
Distinct from prior distillation approaches
[
208
,
209
]
that match student and teacher predictions over a fixed set of classes,
L_{G}
is stochastic and thereby more efficient since it matches the top-
n
item proximity distributions over different sets of sampled items in each task.
The distillation loss
L_{G}
transfers knowledge from
{\bm{R}}_{B}
to the group embeddings
{\mathcal{Z}}_{M}
and is trained jointly with the rest of the framework.

Item Prototype Fusion via Neural Gating

The initial prototype
{\mathbf{p}}_{i}
for item
i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
direct encodes its support users
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
, while the group-enhanced prototype
{\mathbf{g}}_{i}
captures the knowledge transferred from related items (via base recommender
{\bm{R}}_{B}
).
We design a
gating mechanism

[
210
]
that adaptively selects salient feature dimensions from
{\mathbf{p}}_{i}
and
{\mathbf{g}}_{i}
to infer the final
gated item prototype

{\mathbf{e}}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
.
Specifically, we introduce a neural gating layer to merge
{\mathbf{p}}_{i}
and
{\mathbf{g}}_{i}
by learning a
non-linear gate
to flexibly modulate information flow via dimension re-weighting, defined by:

\displaystyle\bf{gate}

\displaystyle=\sigma\big({\bm{W}}_{g_{1}}{\mathbf{p}}_{i}+{\bm{W}}_{g_{2}}{\mathbf{g}}_{i}+{\mathbf{b}}_{g}\big)\quad i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}

\displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\bf{gate}\odot{\bm{p}}_{i}+(1-\bf{gate})\odot{\bm{g}}_{i}

(4.11)

where
{\bm{W}}_{g_{1}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D\times D}
,
{\bm{W}}_{g_{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D\times D}
, and
{\mathbf{b}}_{g}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
are learnable weight parameters in the neural gating layer,
\odot
denotes the element-wise (or hadammard) product operation, and
\sigma
is the sigmoid non-linearity function.

Figure 4.6:
Architecture diagram of
ProtoCF
depicting the different model components: pre-trained neural base recommender
{\bm{R}}_{B}
(top left), group embedding learning via stochastic knowledge distillation
L_{G}
(bottom left), initial item prototype construction via support set averaging followed by group-enrichment and adaptive gating to construct gated item prototype
{\mathbf{e}}_{i}
(right).

Few-shot Recommender Training

We induce few-shot item rankings for the query users
{\mathcal{Q}}
using the gated item prototypes
\{{\mathbf{e}}_{i}:i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}\}
in meta-training task
{\mathcal{T}}
.
We generate item recommendations for each query user
u^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Q}}
(over the task items
{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
) by measuring the similarity
\text{sim}_{m}(\cdot)
with each item prototype
{\mathbf{e}}_{i}
Each meta-training task
{\mathcal{T}}
minimizes a negative log-likelihood
L_{P}
between the few-shot item recommendations for query users
{\mathcal{Q}}
and their ground-truth interactions in
{\mathcal{T}}
, which is defined by:

L_{P}=-\frac{1}{KN}\sum\limits_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}}\sum\limits_{u^{\prime}_{i,k^{\prime}}\in Q_{i}}\log p_{F}(i\mid u^{\prime}_{i,k^{\prime}},\theta)

(4.12)

where
p_{F}(i\mid u^{\prime}_{i,k^{\prime}},\theta)
is computed based on cosine similarity and the choice of likelihood function for few-shot training.

The overall loss
L
is composed of two terms, the few-shot recommendation loss
L_{P}
(Equation
4.12
) and the knowledge distillation loss
L_{G}
for group embedding learning (Equation
4.10
), given by:

L=L_{P}+\lambda L_{G}

(4.13)

where
\lambda
is a tunable hyper-parameter that balances the two loss terms. Algorithm
4.1
summarizes the training procedure of our entire framework
ProtoCF
.

Model Inference

We infer the
gated prototype

{\mathbf{e}}_{i}
for each item
i\in{\mathcal{I}}
by sub-sampling
K
interactions from its historical interactions
N_{i}
as the the support set (Equation
4.11
).
We generate personalized item recommendations for each user
u\in{\mathcal{U}}
by:

\hat{y}_{f}(u,i)=\text{sim}_{m}({\mathbf{e}}_{u},{\mathbf{e}}_{i})\quad i\in{\mathcal{I}}\qquad{\mathbf{e}}_{u}=G_{U}(u,{\bm{X}}\mid\theta)

(4.14)

The few-shot recommender
{\bm{R}}_{F}
is designed for recommendations over tail items (sparse interactions), while the base recommender
{\bm{R}}_{B}
is effective for head items (abundant interactions).
In
ProtoCF
, we compute rankings over the entire item set
{\mathcal{I}}
by ensembling predictions from
{\bm{R}}_{B}
and
{\bm{R}}_{F}
. One simple interpolation approach is given by:

\hat{y}(u,i)=(1-\eta)\cdot\hat{y}_{b}(u,i)+\eta\cdot\hat{y}_{f}(u,i)

(4.15)

where
0<\eta<1
balances
{\bm{R}}_{F}
and
{\bm{R}}_{B}
. We empirically show effective overall item recommendations with
\eta=0.5
.

4.4.3
Model Details

We now discuss different choices of likelihood functions for training the few-shot recommender
{\bm{R}}_{F}
and architectural details of the base recommender
{\bm{R}}_{B}
.

Few-shot Likelihood

We examine two likelihood functions in neural CF models:
multinomial
and
logistic
.

•

Multinomial log-likelihood:

The scores
\text{sim}_{m}({\mathbf{e}}_{u^{\prime}},{\mathbf{e}}_{i})
for each query user
u^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
, over the
N
gated item prototypes in meta-training task
{\mathcal{T}}
, are normalized to produce probabilities
p_{F}(i\mid u^{\prime},\theta)
over the
N
items
{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
, defined by:

p_{F}(i\mid u^{\prime},\theta)=\frac{\exp\big(\text{sim}_{m}({\mathbf{e}}_{u^{\prime}},{\mathbf{e}}_{i})\big)}{\sum_{j\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}}\exp\big(\text{sim}_{m}({\mathbf{e}}_{u^{\prime}},{\mathbf{e}}_{j})\big)}\qquad u^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}

(4.16)

The resulting loss
L_{G}
(with Equation
4.12
) is also known as the
cross-entropy
loss, defined over the
N
items of meta-training task
{\mathcal{T}}
.

•

Logistic log-likelihood:

The relevance
\hat{y}_{u^{\prime}i}=\text{sim}_{m}({\mathbf{e}}_{u^{\prime}},{\mathbf{e}}_{i})
for item
i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
to query user
u^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
, is transformed into a probability using the sigmoid function
\sigma
.
We use a
confidence
weight
\beta>0
to re-weight the likelihood of observed 1’s which are far fewer than the unobserved 0’s in implicit feedback. The logistic log-likelihood is given by:

\log p_{F}(i\mid u^{\prime},\theta)=\beta\log\sigma(\hat{y}_{u^{\prime}i})+\sum\limits_{j\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N},u^{\prime}\notin N_{j}}\log(1-\sigma(\hat{y}_{u^{\prime}j}))

(4.17)

Algorithm 4.1

ProtoCF
: Prototypical Collaborative Filtering

1:

User-item interactions
{\bm{X}}
, meta-training task distribution
p({\mathbb{T}})
.

2:

Function
f_{T}(i\mid u),i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{T}
for few-shot item recommendations.

3:

Pre-train the neural base recommender
{\bm{R}}_{B}
on the training interactions
{\bm{X}}
to learn
f_{H,\phi}(i\mid u)
(eqn
4.2
) and freeze parameters
\phi
.

4:

Initialize user preference encoder
G_{U}(\cdot\mid\theta)
of few-shot recommender
{\bm{R}}_{F}
with parameters
F_{U}(\cdot\mid\phi)
from the base recommender
{\bm{R}}_{B}
.

5:

\triangleright

Meta-training: learn-to-recommend few-shot items.

6:

while

not converged

do

7:

Sample a meta-training task
{\mathcal{T}}=\{I_{{\mathcal{T}},N},{\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{Q}}\}\sim p({\mathbb{T}})
.

8:

Calculate the initial
{\mathbf{p}}_{i}
and group-enhanced
{\mathbf{g}}_{i}
prototypes for items
i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
(eqn
4.5
and eqn
4.7
).

9:

Compute the knowledge distillation loss
L_{G}
(eqn
4.10
).

10:

Compute gated prototype
{\mathbf{e}}_{i}
for items
i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{{\mathcal{T}},N}
(eqn
4.11
).

11:

Estimate few-shot recommendation loss
L_{P}
(eqn
4.12
) with
multinomial
(eqn
4.16
) or
logistic
(eqn
4.17
) log-likelihoods.

12:

Minimize overall loss
L
(eqn
4.13
) using mini-batch gradient descent.

13:

\triangleright

Model Inference: few-shot and overall item recommendations
.

14:

Generate item recommendations over
{\mathcal{I}}
for user
u\in{\mathcal{U}}
(eqn
4.15
)

The cosine similarity scores are also scaled to match the non-saturating regimes of the softmax and sigmoid functions in the multinomial and logistic log-likelihoods respectively.

Neural Base Recommender Architecture

We consider three neural collaborative filtering methods, matrix factorization (MF)
[
87
]
, denoising (CDAE)
[
89
]
and variational (VAE-CF)
[
90
]
autoencoders, as base recommenders
{\bm{R}}_{B}
(Equation
4.1
) for our framework
ProtoCF
. We briefly describe their architectures:

•

Matrix Factorization (MF)
[
87
]
: The user
F_{U}
and item
F_{I}
encoders are learnable latent embeddings for each user and item respectively, which are trained using a logistic log-likelihood training objective.

•

Variational AutoEncoder (VAE-CF)
[
90
]
: The user encoder
F_{U}
is a two-layer Multi-Layer Perceptron transforming the binary user preference vector
{\mathbf{x}}_{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{I}
into a
D
-dimensional user embedding
{\bm{h}}_{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, defined by:

{\bm{h}}_{u}=F_{U}({\bm{x}}_{u}\mid\phi)=\sigma({\bm{W}}_{2}^{T}(\sigma({\bm{W}}_{1}^{T}{\bm{x}}_{u}+b_{1})+b_{2})

(4.18)

with learnable weight matrices
{\bm{W}}_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{I_{H}\times D}
and
{\bm{W}}_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D\times D}
, biases
b_{1},b_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, and
tanh(\cdot)
activations for non-linearity
\sigma
.
The item encoder
F_{I}
is a latent embedding. VAE-CF uses a multinomial likelihood with KL-divergence regularization.

•

Denoising AutoEncoders (CDAE)
[
89
]
: The user encoder
F_{U}
operates on partially corrupted inputs
{\mathbf{x}}_{u}
and adds an auxiliary per-user embedding to the encoder output from Equation
4.18
.
We train CDAE using a multinomial log-likelihood, albeit without the KL-divergence prior regularization.

4.5
Experiments

We present experiments on four real-world datasets to evaluate our framework
ProtoCF
. We introduce datasets, baselines and experimental setup in Sections

4.5.1
,
4.5.2
and
4.5.3
.
We propose four research questions to guide our experiments:

(
\textbf{RQ}_{\textbf{1}}
)

Does
ProtoCF
beat state-of-the-art NCF and sparsity-aware methods on
overall
recommendation performance?

(
\textbf{RQ}_{\textbf{2}}
)

What is the impact of item
interaction sparsity
on the
few-shot
recommendation performance of
ProtoCF
?

(
\textbf{RQ}_{\textbf{3}}
)

How do the different
architectural
choices impact the few-shot and overall performance of
ProtoCF
?

(
\textbf{RQ}_{\textbf{4}}
)

How do the
hyper-parameters
(distillation loss balance factor
\lambda
and meta-training task size
N
) affect
ProtoCF
?

Finally, we discuss the limitations of our
ProtoCF
model and explore future directions in Section
4.5.8
.

4.5.1
Datasets

We conducted experiments on four publicly available benchmark datasets, including two online product review platforms (
Epinions
,
Yelp
) and two check-in based social networks (
Weeplaces
,
Gowalla
).

•

Epinions
1
1

1

https://www.cse.msu.edu/ tangjili/datasetcode/truststudy.htm
:
product ratings from an e-commerce platform;
we retain interactions with ratings higher than two.

•

Yelp
2
2

2

https://www.yelp.com/dataset
:
user ratings on local businesses located in the state of Arizona, obtained from Yelp dataset challenge round 13.

•

Weeplaces
3
3

3

https://www.yongliu.org/datasets/
:
we extract business check-ins from Weeplaces of different categories, including Nightlife, Outdoors, Entertainment, Travel and Food, across all cities in the United States.

•

Gowalla
[
211
]
:
restaurant check-ins in Gowalla by users across different cities in the United States.

We pre-process the datasets to retain users and items with at least ten interactions using the 10-core setting
[
212
]
(Table
4.3
).

4.5.2
Baselines

We present comparisons against prior work that broadly fall into two categories:
standard
neural collaborative filtering
models, and
sparsity-aware long-tail item recommendation
models, including regularization and meta-learning techniques.

•

Neural Base Recommenders
: neural CF methods with matrix factorization (BPR)
[
87
]
, and autoencoder models VAE-CF
[
90
]
and CDAE
[
89
]
(described in Section
4.4.3
).

•

NCF
[
88
]
: neural CF model with non-linear interactions (via neural layers) between the user and item embeddings.

•

NGCF
[
91
]
: state-of-the-art graph-based NCF with embedding propagation layers on the user-item interaction graph.

•

Cofactor
[
193
]
: regularized MF to capture inter-item associations by jointly decomposing the user-item interaction matrix and the item-item co-occurrence matrix, with shared latent item factors.

•

EFM
[
194
]
: embedding factorization model that uses item-item co-occurrence with bayesian personalized ranking.

•

DropoutNet
[
108
]
:
randomly dropout latent CF embedding for regularization in content-based CF for cold-start (auxiliary content). We adapt it to long-tail items by replacing content embeddings with prototypes (over support set).

•

MetaRec-LWA

[
116
]
meta-learning method to construct recommendation models for cold-start users with user-specific linear transforms; we adapt it to construct few-shot item embeddings.

•

MetaRec-NLBA

[
116
]
: meta-learning recommendation model that learns user-specific biases parameterized by non-linear layers, to replace the user-specific weights in MetaRec-LWA.

Note that we omit experimental baseline comparisons with gradient-based meta-learning recommenders
[
110
,
112
]
, since they are designed for cold-start recommendation in the presence of auxiliary attributes.
We test
ProtoCF
by adopting BPR
[
87
]
(matrix factorization), VAE-CF
[
90
]
, and CDAE
[
89
]
as base recommenders of our framework.

4.5.3
Experimental Setup

In each dataset, we randomly split the user interactions of all items into train (70%) and test (30%) sets, which ensures consistent interaction distributions across train and test sets.
We also use 10% of training interactions as validation for hyper-parameter tuning.
We use NDCG@K and Recall@K as evaluation metrics, computed based on the rank of ground-truth test interactions in top-
K
ranked item lists.

We design a unified model to enhance few-shot recommendation on the tail without affecting the overall performance.
We first evaluate
overall
recommendations over the entire item set
{\mathcal{I}}
followed by
few-shot
performance analysis over the long-tail.

All experiments are conducted on a Tesla K-80 GPU using PyTorch.
ProtoCF
is trained using episodic learning for a maximum of 300 episodes with a meta-training task size of 512 items using Adam optimizer.

ProtoCF
uses a multinomial log-likelihood for few-shot training and uses support sets of upto
K=10
and query sets of
K^{\prime}=5
interactions per item.
We learn
M=100
group embeddings
{\bm{Z}}_{M}
, tune the learning rate and balance hyper-parameter
\lambda
(Equation
4.13
) in the range
\{10^{-4},10^{-3},10^{-2}\}
, and use dropout regularization with a rate of 0.5.
We tune baselines in hyper-parameter ranges centered at the author-provided values on each dataset and set the latent embedding dimension to 128 for consistency.
Our implementation of
ProtoCF
and datasets are publicly available
4
4

4

https://github.com/aravindsankar28/ProtoCF
.

4.5.4
Overall Recommendation Results (
\text{RQ}_{1}
)

Our experimental results comparing the
overall
recommendation performance of
ProtoCF
with competing baselines are shown in Table
4.4
. We summarize our key empirical observations below:

•

Neural CF models based on autoencoders (VAE-CF, CDAE) and graph neural networks (NGCF) outperform other latent-factor models (NCF, BPR) on overall model performance (Table
4.4
).

•

Model regularization strategies that use item co-occurrence information (CoFactor, EFM) to improve long-tail item recommendations, are noticeably worse than BPR in overall performance.

•

Sparsity-aware meta-learning models (MetaRec) perform poorly in overall item rankings.
One potential reason is their inability to effectively exploit or transfer knowledge from head items.

•

ProtoCF
outperforms state-of-the-art baselines (NDCG@50 gains of 5% on average) on
overall
item ranking, with consistent gains for the variants of all neural base recommenders.

Next, we examine the few-shot performance of
ProtoCF
.

4.5.5
Few-Shot Recommendation Results (
\text{RQ}_{2}
)

To evaluate few-shot results, we qualitatively analyze performance for long-tail items with sparse interactions. Specifically, we compare recommendation results for long-tail items with varying number of training interactions
K
(5 to 30) by computing Recall@50 metrics only on their associated test interactions (Figure
4.7
).
We only include the base recommenders BPR and VAE-CF here for comparison since they consistently outperform other sparsity-aware variants.

We find that model performance generally increases with item interaction count;
ProtoCF
achieves significant performance gains for items with less than 20 interactions.
Episodic training with knowledge transfer is one of the key factors responsible for our higher gains over items with sparse interactions (small values of
K
).

Figure 4.7:
Few-shot item recommendation results: Performance comparison for long-tail items with varying number of training interactions
K
(5 to 30); lines denote model performance (Recall@50) and background histograms indicate the cumulative fraction of the item inventory covered by tail items with
\leq K
impressions. Overall performance generally increases with
K
for all models;
ProtoCF
achieves notably stronger gains (over baselines) for items with few training interactions (small
K
).

To evaluate the impact of
interaction sparsity
across the entire item set, we compare overall recommendation performance (Recall@K) across item-groups with different sparsity levels.
We divide the test set into ten equal-sized item-groups, sorted in increasing order by the average number of interactions per item.
Figure
4.8
compares
ProtoCF
against two base recommenders BPR and VAE-CF.

From figure
4.8
, model performance is lower on the
long-tail
for base recommenders BPR and VAE due to severe interaction sparsity.
Notably,
ProtoCF
achieves much higher item recall scores on the
tail
items with significant gains over the corresponding base recommenders, while ensuring comparable performance on the
head
items. Knowledge transfer of
item-to-item relationships
via group embeddings and pre-trained user encoder enables
ProtoCF
to learn discriminative prototypes for tail items with sparse interactions.

Figure 4.8:
Impact of item interaction sparsity: Performance comparison for item-groups sorted in increasing order by their average training interaction counts; lines denote model performance (Recall@50) and background histograms indicate the average number of interactions in each item-group.
ProtoCF
has significant performance gains (over baselines) on the tail items (item-groups
G_{1}
to
G_{8}
) while maintaining comparable performance on the head items (item-groups
G_{9}
to
G_{10}
).

4.5.6
Model Ablation Study (
\text{RQ}_{3}
)

We examine the impact of different
architectural
design choices in
ProtoCF
on its
overall
and
few-shot
performance (Table
4.5
). We choose VAE-CF as the base recommender to instantiate
ProtoCF
due to its consistent results. Here, we report few-shot performance by only considering the test interactions of
long-tail
items with less than 20 training interactions.
Note that most ablation variants do not have a significant impact on overall results since all predictions are computed by ensembling
{\bm{R}}_{F}
and
{\bm{R}}_{B}
(Equation
4.15
).
Our key design choices and empirical insights are shown below:
